\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P
and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash Q

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P\lor Q

iff

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P
or
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash Q

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P\to Q

iff

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash P
implies
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash Q
for all
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\sqsupseteq\mathcal{P}

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P*Q

iff

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{P}\vDash P
and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{Q}\vDash Q
for some

\mathcal{P}_{P}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{Q}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{P}

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P-\!\!*~Q

iff

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{P}\vDash P
implies

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{P}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\vDash Q
for all
\mathcal{P}_{P}
with
\mathcal{P}_{P}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}
defined

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\operatorname{\square}P

iff

\gamma,D,1\vDash P

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\forall x\mathrm{:}S.P

iff

(\gamma,x),D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P
for all
x\in S

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\exists x\mathrm{:}S.P

iff

(\gamma,x),D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P
for some
x\in S

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.P

iff

\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}\vDash P
for all

X:\operatorname{RV}A

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.P

iff

\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}\vDash P
for some

X:\operatorname{RV}A

\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)\vDash E\sim\mu^{\prime}

iff

E(\gamma)\circ D
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable and

\mu^{\prime}(\gamma)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(E(\gamma)(D(\omega)))\end{aligned}~\right)

\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E

iff

E(\gamma)\circ D
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable

\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)\vDash E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}

iff

F\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F)=1
and
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\mathrm{cod}(X_{1})\otimes\mathrm{cod}(X_{2})

where
F=\{\omega\mid X_{1}(\omega)=X_{2}(\omega)\}

and
X_{i}=E_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
for
i\in\{1,2\}

\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)\vDash\Ex[E]=e

iff

E(\gamma)\circ D
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable and

\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}[E(\gamma)(D(\omega))]=e(\gamma)

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)

iff

for all
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{frame}}
and
\mu

with
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and all
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}{\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket}

there exists
X:\operatorname{RV}A
and
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}
with

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu^{\prime})

such that

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash Q

Proof.

We must verify that the extended random substitutions

(D,X)
in the interpretations of
\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}
,
\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}
, and
\mathsf{wp}

have finite footprint; in all cases this follows from the fact that
D
and
X
have finite footprint.
∎

Lemma B.7 (separating conjunction is mutual independence).

Fix a configuration
(\gamma,D,\mathcal{P})
.
Abbreviating
X_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
as
X_{i}^{\prime}
,
random variables
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}
are mutually independent with respect to
\mathcal{P}

iff
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X_{1}*\dots*\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X_{n}
.

Proof.

First suppose
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X_{1}*\dots*\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X_{n}
, so
each
X_{i}^{\prime}
is
\mathcal{P}_{i}
-measurable for some
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{P}
.
Write
\mathcal{P}=(\mathcal{F},\mu)
and
\mathcal{P}_{i}=(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mu_{i})
for all
1\leq i\leq n
.
For any subset
J
of
\{1,\dots,n\}
and any collection of events

\{E_{j}\in\mathcal{F}_{j}\}_{j\in J}
,
we have

\Pr\left[\bigwedge_{j\in J}X_{j}^{\prime}\in E_{j}\right]=\mu\left(\bigcap_{j\in J}X_{j}^{\prime-1}(E_{j})\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\prod_{j\in J}\mu_{j}(X_{j}^{\prime-1}(E_{j}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\prod_{j\in J}\mu(X_{j}^{\prime-1}(E_{j}))=\prod_{j\in J}\Pr[X_{j}^{\prime}\in E_{j}]

where
(a)
and
(b)
hold because
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{P}

and
X_{j}^{\prime-1}(E_{j})\in\mathcal{F}_{j}
for all
j
. Hence
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}
are mutually independent.

For the converse, suppose
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{n}^{\prime}
are mutually independent with respect to some probability
space
\mathcal{P}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq n
, let
\mathcal{P}_{i}
be the probability space
(\mathcal{F}_{i},\mu_{i})

where
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is the pullback
\sigma
-algebra along
X_{i}^{\prime}
and
\mu_{i}
the restriction of
\mu
to

\mathcal{F}_{i}
.
It’s enough to show that the composition

\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}
is defined, as
then
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{P}
by lemma

2.1
.
This follows by induction on
n
.
Cases
n=0
and
n=1
are immediate.
Now suppose
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{k}
is defined.
It’s straightforward to show that
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{k}
is the pullback of the random variable

(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime})
,
and
\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
is the pullback of
X_{k+1}^{\prime}
by definition.
Mutual independence of
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime},X_{k+1}^{\prime}

implies independence of
(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime})
and
X_{k+1}^{\prime}
: intersections of
events
X_{1}^{\prime-1}(E_{1})\cap\dots\cap X_{k}^{\prime-1}(E_{k})
form a
\pi
-system that
generates
\mathcal{F}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{F}_{k}
,
events independent of
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}
with respect to
\mu
form a
\lambda
-system,
and each intersection
X_{1}^{\prime-1}(E_{1})\cap\dots\cap X_{k}^{\prime-1}(E_{k})
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{k+1}

because
X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime},X_{k+1}^{\prime}
are mutually independent.
Thus the pullback of
(X_{1}^{\prime},\dots,X_{k}^{\prime},X_{k+1}^{\prime})
is an independent combination
of
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{k}
and
\mathcal{P}_{k+1}
, and
\mathcal{P}_{1}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{k}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{k+1}

is defined.
This closes the induction, so
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X_{1}^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\dots\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}X_{n}^{\prime}
as desired.
∎

B.4.1.
Substitution

Substitutions take the form
(s,S)
where
s
is a substitution
of deterministic values and
S
a substitution of random variables.

\frac{s\in\llbracket\Gamma^{\prime}\rrbracket\to\llbracket\Gamma\rrbracket\quad S\in\llbracket\Delta^{\prime}\rrbracket\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{m}\,}}}{{\to}}\llbracket\Delta\rrbracket}{\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash(s,S):\Gamma;\Delta}

\framebox{$\frac{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash_{{\textrm{rv}}}E:A\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash(s,S):\Gamma;\Delta}{\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash E[s,S]:A}$}

E[s,S](\gamma)=E(s(\gamma))\circ S

\framebox{$\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{{\textrm{det}}}e:A\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:\Gamma}{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash e[s]:A}$}

e[s]=e\circ s

\framebox{$\frac{\Gamma\vdash_{{\textrm{det}}}\mu:A\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash s:\Gamma}{\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\mu[s]:A}$}

\mu[s]=\mu\circ s

\framebox{$\frac{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash_{{\textrm{prog}}}M:A\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash(s,S):\Gamma;\Delta}{\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash M[s,S]:A}$}

M[s,S](\gamma)=M(s(\gamma))\circ S

\framebox{$\frac{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\hskip 8.04996pt\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash(s,S):\Gamma;\Delta}{\Gamma^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash P[s,S]}$}

\begin{aligned} \top[s,S]&=\top\\
\bot[s,S]&=\bot\\
(P\land Q)[s,S]&=(P[s,S]\land Q[s,S])\\
(P\lor Q)[s,S]&=(P[s,S]\lor Q[s,S])\\
(P\to Q)[s,S]&=(P[s,S]\to Q[s,S])\\
(P*Q)[s,S]&=(P[s,S]*Q[s,S])\\
(P-\!\!*~Q)[s,S]&=(P[s,S]-\!\!*~Q[s,S])\\
(\operatorname{\square}P)[s,S]&=\operatorname{\square}P[s,S]\\
(\forall x\mathrm{:}T.P)[s,S]&=\forall x\mathrm{:}T.P[s\times 1_{T},S]\\
(\exists x\mathrm{:}T.P)[s,S]&=\exists x\mathrm{:}T.P[s\times 1_{T},S]\\
(\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.P)[s,S]&=\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.P[s,S\times 1_{A}]\\
(\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.P)[s,S]&=\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.P[s,S\times 1_{A}]\\
(E\sim\mu)[s,S]&=E[s,S]\sim\mu[s]\\
(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E)[s,S]&=\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E[s,S]\\
(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})[s,S]&=E_{1}[s,S]\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}[s,S]\\
(\Ex[E]=e)[s,S]&=\Ex[E[s,S]]=e[s]\\
\mathsf{wp}(M,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)[s,S]&=\mathsf{wp}(M[s,S],X\mathrm{:}A.Q[s,S\times 1_{A}])\end{aligned}

Lemma B.8 (syntactic and semantic substitution coincide).

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P[s,S]
iff
s(\gamma),S\circ D,\mathcal{P}\vDash P
.

Proof.

By induction on the syntax of propositions.
The interesting cases are:

•

Case
E\sim\mu
:

\displaystyle\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash(E\sim\mu)[s,S]

\displaystyle\textrm{iff }\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash((\gamma\mapsto E(\gamma)\circ S)\sim(\mu\circ S))

\displaystyle\textrm{iff }E(s(\gamma))\circ S\circ D\textrm{ is }\mathcal{P}\textrm{-measurable and }\mu(\gamma)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mathcal{P}\\
&\mathrm{ret}~E(\gamma)(S(D(\omega)))\end{aligned}~\right)

\displaystyle\textrm{iff }s(\gamma),S\circ D,\mathcal{P}\vDash E\sim\mu

•

Case
\mathsf{wp}(M,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)
:
For the left-to-right direction,
suppose (1)
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M[s,S],X\mathrm{:}A.Q[s,S\times 1_{A}])

and (2)
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and (3)
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}{\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket}
.
By (1) there exist
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
and
\mu^{\prime}
with

\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu^{\prime})

and
X:\operatorname{RV}A
such that

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M[s,S](\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}\vDash Q[s,S\times 1_{A}]
.
By IH this is equivalent to
s(\gamma),(S\circ D,X),\mathcal{P}\vDash Q

and simplifying the above equation gives

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M(s(\gamma))(S(D(\omega)));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

Now postcomposing both sides with the map

(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta,v)\mapsto(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},S(\delta),v)

gives

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M(s(\gamma))(S(D(\omega)));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},S(D(\omega)),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},S(D(\omega)),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

so that
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},X)
witnesses
s(\gamma),S\circ D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)
as
desired.

For the right-to-left direction, suppose
(1)
s(\gamma),S\circ D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)

and (2)
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and (3)
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}{\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket}
.
Specialize (1) with
D_{\textrm{ext}}:=(D_{\textrm{ext}},D)

to get
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
,
\mu^{\prime}
and
X:\operatorname{RV}A
such that

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M(s(\gamma))(S(D(\omega)));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~((D_{\textrm{ext}},D),S(D(\omega)),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~((D_{\textrm{ext}},D),S(D(\omega)),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
s(\gamma),(S\circ D,X),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash Q
.
By IH this is equivalent to
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash Q[s,S\times 1_{A}]
,
and postcomposing both sides of the above equation with the map

((\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta),\_,v)\mapsto(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta,v)

and rewriting
M(s(\gamma))(S(D(\omega)))
in terms of
M[s,S]
gives

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M[s,S](\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},D,v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}},D,X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

so that
(\mathcal{P}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},X)
witnesses

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M[s,S],X\mathrm{:}A.Q[s,S\times 1_{A}])

as desired.

∎

B.5.
Properties of almost-sure equality

Definition B.8 (spaces that support equality).

Say that a measurable space
(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})

supports equality

if the diagonal
\Delta_{\mathbf{A}}:=\{(a,a)\mid a\in\mathbf{A}\}
is measurable in
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
.
This includes all Hausdorff spaces, in particular all of the examples
considered in this paper.

Lemma B.9.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
be a probability space,
F
an event in
\mathcal{F}
,

(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})
a measurable space that supports equality,
and
X_{1},X_{2}:\Omega\to\mathbf{A}
random variables.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

For all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
it holds that
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
.

(2)

For all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
it holds that
F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
.

(3)

F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
,
where
(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})
denotes the pullback
\sigma
-algebra
and
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})
is defined to be
the set
\{F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\mid A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}\}
.

(4)

F\cup X_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
and
F\cup X_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.

Proof.

(1) and (3) are equivalent by definition.
(1) and (2) are equivalent because
\mathcal{F}
is closed under complements
and preimages
(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)
are in bijection with their complements

{(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)}^{c}
via
{(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)}^{c}=(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A^{c})
.
This establishes the equivalence of (1), (2), and (3).
Finally, (3) implies (4)
because
(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})
contains both
X_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})

and
X_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})
, so it only remains to show (4) implies (3).
Suppose (4) with the goal of showing

F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
.
First note that if
A
is of the form
A_{1}\times A_{2}
for some
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}

then

\displaystyle F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A_{1}\times A_{2})

\displaystyle=F\cup(X_{1}^{-1}(A_{1})\cap X_{2}^{-1}(A_{2}))=\underbrace{(F\cup X_{1}^{-1}(A_{1}))}_{\in\mathcal{F}\text{ by (4)}}\cap\underbrace{(F\cup X_{2}^{-1}(A_{2}))}_{\in\mathcal{F}\text{ by (4)}}

from which the result follows because
\mathcal{F}
is closed under finite intersections.
Next note that the set of events
A
for which
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
forms a
\sigma
-algebra:

•

F\cup\emptyset=F\in\mathcal{F}
,

•

If
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
then
F\cup((X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))^{c}\in\mathcal{F}
because

\displaystyle F\cup((X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))^{c}

\displaystyle=\Omega\cap(F\cup((X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))^{c})

\displaystyle=(F\cup F^{c})\cap(F\cup((X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))^{c})

\displaystyle=F\cup(F^{c}\cap((X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))^{c})

\displaystyle=F\cup{\underbrace{(F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))}_{\in\mathcal{F}}}^{c}

from which the result follows because
\mathcal{F}
is closed under complements and finite unions.

•

If
\{A_{i}\}
is a countable family with
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A_{i})\in\mathcal{F}

for all
i
, then

F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\bigcup_{i}A_{i})=\bigcup_{i}(F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A_{i}))\in\mathcal{F}
because
\mathcal{F}

is closed under countable unions.

Together these two points imply that the set of events
A
for which
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}

is a
\sigma
-algebra containing all measurable boxes
A_{1}\times A_{2}
for
A_{1},A_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
.
Since
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
is the smallest
\sigma
-algebra containing all such boxes,
we have
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
as desired.
∎

Lemma B.10 (almost-sure equality is an equivalence relation).

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})
be a measurable space that supports equality.
Let
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
be random expressions of type
\mathbf{A}
.
The following entailments hold:

\frac{}{\vdash E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{1}}\quad\textsc{lab=Refl}\frac{}{E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}\vdash E_{2}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{1}}\quad\textsc{lab=Sym}\frac{}{E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}\land E_{2}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{3}\vdash E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{3}}\quad\textsc{lab=Trans}

Proof.

Fix a configuration
(\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu))
. Define

X_{i}=E_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}

and let
F_{ij}
be the event
\{\omega\mid X_{i}(\omega)=X_{j}(\omega)\}

that
X_{i}
and
X_{j}
are equal.

•

Refl
: we need to show
F_{11}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F_{11})=1
,
and that
F_{11}\cup(X_{1},X_{1})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
.
By definition
F_{11}=\{\omega\mid X_{1}(\omega)=X_{1}(\omega)\}=\Omega
,
and because
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
is a probability space, we have that

\Omega\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(\Omega)=1
.
Since
F_{11}=\Omega
, we have
F_{11}\cup F^{\prime}=F_{11}\in\mathcal{F}
for all
F^{\prime}
,
so
F_{11}\cup(X_{1},X_{1})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A
as required.

•

Sym
: we have
F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}

and
\mu(F_{12})=1
and
F_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
,
and need
F_{21}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F_{21})=1
and
F_{21}\cup(X_{2},X_{1})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
.
This follows from
F_{12}=F_{21}
and the fact that preimages
(X_{2},X_{1})^{-1}(A)

are in bijective correspondence with preimages
(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)
by

(X_{2},X_{1})^{-1}(A)=(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathrm{swap}(A))
where
\mathrm{swap}(x,y)=(y,x)
.

•

Trans
: by Lemma
B.9
, we have

(1)

F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F_{12})=1
and
F_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}

(2)

F_{23}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F_{23})=1
and
F_{23}\cup(X_{2},X_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}

and need
F_{13}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F_{13})=1
and
F_{13}\cup(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.

–

F_{13}\in\mathcal{F}
:
note that
F_{13}=(F_{12}\cap F_{23})\uplus(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap F_{13})
;
unwinding the notation, this states the following equivalence of events:

X_{1}=X_{3}\iff(X_{1}=X_{2}\land X_{2}=X_{3})\lor(X_{1}\neq X_{2}\land X_{2}\neq X_{3}\land X_{1}=X_{3})

Since
\mathcal{F}
is closed under finite unions and intersections, we are done if we can show that

F_{12}
,
F_{23}
, and
(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap F_{13})
are in
\mathcal{F}
.
By (1) and (2) and Lemma
B.9
we have that
\mathcal{F}
contains

F_{12}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\qquad F_{12}^{c}\cap X_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\qquad F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{2}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\qquad F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})

We have
\mathbf{A}\in\mathcal{A}
because
\mathcal{A}
is a
\sigma
-algebra,
so
\mathcal{F}
also contains
F_{12}^{c}
and
F_{23}^{c}
.
By closure under intersections
\mathcal{F}
also contains

F_{23}^{c}\cap(F_{12}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}))\qquad F_{12}^{c}\cap(F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(\mathcal{A}))

Now by Lemma
B.9
again, we have that
\mathcal{F}
contains

F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})
.
In particular, since
\mathcal{A}
supports equality, we have
\Delta_{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
,
so
F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathbf{A}})\in\mathcal{F}
.
Now
(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathbf{A}})=F_{13}
by definition,
so
F^{c}_{12}\cap F^{c}_{23}\cap F_{13}\in\mathcal{F}
.
Along the way we have shown
F_{12}^{c}\in\mathcal{F}
and
F_{23}^{c}\in\mathcal{F}
,
which implies
F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
and
F_{23}\in\mathcal{F}
by closure under complements,
as required.

–

\mu(F_{13})=1
: we have
F_{12}\cap F_{23}\subseteq F_{13}

(by transitivity of equality on the functions
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
)
and
\mu(F_{12})=\mu(F_{23})=1
by assumption. Thus

\displaystyle\mu(F_{13})\geq\mu(F_{12}\cap F_{23})=1-\mu(F_{12}^{c}\cup F_{23}^{c})\geq 1-(\mu(F_{12}^{c})+\mu(F_{23}^{c}))=1

as required.

–

F_{13}\cup(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
:
by Lemma
B.9
it suffices to show

F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
and

F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.
To this end fix arbitrary
A\in\mathcal{A}
with aim to show

F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
and
F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
.

*

F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
:
note that

F_{13}^{c}=(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23})\uplus(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap F_{13}^{c})\uplus(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c})
;
unwinding the notation, this states the following equivalence of events:

X_{1}\neq X_{3}\iff\begin{aligned} &(X_{1}\neq X_{2}=X_{3})\\
&\lor~(X_{1}\neq X_{2}\land X_{2}\neq X_{3}\land X_{1}\neq X_{3})\\
&\lor~(X_{1}=X_{2}\neq X_{3})\end{aligned}

Thus the intersection
F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A)
can be rewritten as the following union:

(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A))\uplus(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A))\uplus(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A))

It only remains to show that each component of this union is in
\mathcal{F}
.
As in the proof of
F_{13}\in\mathcal{F}
above, we have that
\mathcal{F}
contains
each of the following:

F_{12}\qquad F_{23}\qquad F_{13}\qquad(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c})\cap(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})

The first component
F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A)
is
an intersection of two events

F_{23}
and
F_{12}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A)
that are in
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
Similarly the second component
is equal to
F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\cap(A\times\mathbf{A}))
,
an event in
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
Finally, to show the third component

(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A))

is in
\mathcal{F}
note that we have the equality

(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{1}^{-1}(A))=(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{2}^{-1}(A))

corresponding to the following equivalence of events:

X_{1}=X_{2}\neq X_{3}\land X_{1}\in A\iff X_{1}=X_{2}\neq X_{3}\land X_{2}\in A

Thus the third component is an intersection of events

F_{12}
and
F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{2}^{-1}(A)
that are in
\mathcal{F}

by assumption.

*

F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
:
this case is symmetrical to the one above, with
X_{3}
replaced by
X_{1}
.
The strategy is the same:
note that

F_{13}^{c}=(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23})\uplus(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap F_{13}^{c})\uplus(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c})
,
so the intersection
F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A)
can be rewritten as:

(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A))\uplus(F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap F_{13}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A))\uplus(F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A))

The first component
F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A)
is
equivalent to
F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}\cap X_{2}^{-1}(A)
,
due to the following equivalence of events:

X_{1}\neq X_{2}=X_{3}\land X_{3}\in A\iff X_{1}\neq X_{2}=X_{3}\land X_{2}\in A

Thus the first component is an intersection of events

F_{23}
and
F_{12}^{c}\cap X_{2}^{-1}(A)
that are in
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
The second component
is equal to

F_{12}^{c}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{3})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathbf{A}}^{c}\cap(\mathbf{A}\times A))
,
an event in
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
Finally, the third component
F_{12}\cap F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A)
is
an intersection of two events

F_{12}
and
F_{23}^{c}\cap X_{3}^{-1}(A)
that are in
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.

∎

Lemma B.11.

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mu)
be a probability space and
G
a collection of
full sets. (A full set is an event with probability
1
.)
For all
E\in\langle G\rangle
it holds that
\mu(E)=0

or
\mu(E)=1
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality we may assume that
G
is a
\pi
-system:
if
G
is empty then
\langle G\rangle
is the trivial
\sigma
-algebra and we are done;
if
G
is nonempty, it generates the same
\sigma
-algebra as its
closure under finite intersections, and finite intersections of full sets
remain full.
By the
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem, we are done if we can show that the collection
of events
E
for which
\mu(E)=0
or
\mu(E)=1
is a
\lambda
-system
that contains
G
.

•

Contains
G
: if
E\in G
then
\mu(E)=1
by assumption.

•

Contains
\emptyset
:
\mu(\emptyset)=0
because
\mu
is a measure.

•

Closed under complements: if
\mu(E)\in\{0,1\}
then
\mu(E^{c})=1-\mu(E)\in\{0,1\}
.

•

Closed under countable disjoint unions: let
\{E_{i}\}_{i}
be a pairwise-disjoint
countable family of sets for which
\mu(E_{i})\in\{0,1\}

for all
i
. By countable additivity of measures,

\mu(\biguplus_{i}E_{i})=\sum_{i}\mu(E_{i})\in\mathbb{N}
.
This combined with the fact that
\mu
is a probability measure forces

\mu(\biguplus_{i}E_{i})\in\{0,1\}
as required.

∎

Lemma B.12.

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})
be a measurable space that supports equality,
and let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be random expressions of type
\mathbf{A}
.
The following double-entailment holds:

P\land(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})\dashv\vdash P*(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})

Proof.

The right-to-left entailment follows from the fact that our separation logic is
affine. For the left-to-right entailment, fix a configuration
\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)
,
let
X_{i}=E_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
for
i\in\{1,2\}
, let
F_{12}
be the event
X_{1}=X_{2}
,
and suppose that

(1)

\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)\vDash P

(2)

F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F)=1

(3)

F_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}

with the aim of showing
\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)\vDash P*(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})
.
Let
\mathcal{G}
be the sub-
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathcal{F}

generated by
F_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})
,
and let
\nu
be the restriction of
\mathcal{F}
to
\mathcal{G}
.
The probability space
(\mathcal{G},\nu)
witnesses
E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}
by (2) and (3).
By (1), we are done if we can show
(\mathcal{F},\mu)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}(\mathcal{G},\nu)=(\mathcal{F},\mu)
.
By definition of independent combination, it suffices to show

\mu(F\cap G)=\mu(F)\nu(G)
for all
F\in\mathcal{F}
and
G\in\mathcal{G}
.
Since
\nu
is defined as a restriction of
\mu
to
\mathcal{G}
, this reduces
to showing
\mu(F\cap G)=\mu(F)\mu(G)
.
The
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{G}
is generated by events
F_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})

that are all full, so by Lemma
B.11

we have that
\mu(G)\in\{0,1\}
for all
G\in\mathcal{G}
. There are thus two cases:

•

If
\mu(G)=0
, then
\mu(F\cap G)=0=\mu(F)\mu(G)
as required.

•

If
\mu(G)=1
, then
\mu(F\cap G)=\mu(F)-\mu(F\setminus G)=\mu(F)-0=\mu(F)\mu(G)
as required.

∎

Corollary B.13 (almost-sure equality is duplicable).

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})
be a measurable space that supports equality,
and let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be random expressions of type
\mathbf{A}
.
The following entailment holds:

E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}\vdash(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})*(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})

Proof.

E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}\vdash(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})\land(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{lem:asequal-good-conjunct}}}{{\vdash}}(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})*(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})
.
∎

Lemma B.14 (transfer of ownership).

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})
be a measurable space that supports equality.
Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be random expressions of type
\mathbf{A}
.
The following entailments hold:

\frac{}{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E_{1}\land(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E_{2}}\quad\textsc{lab=Transfer-Own}\frac{}{(E_{1}\sim\nu)\land(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})\vdash E_{2}\sim\nu}\quad\textsc{lab=Transfer-Dist}

Proof.

We prove
Transfer-Dist
; the proof of
Transfer-Own
is identical.
Fix a configuration
(\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu))
,
let
X_{i}=E_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
for
i\in\{1,2\}
,
let
F_{12}
be the event
X_{1}=X_{2}
,
and suppose

(1)

X_{1}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable with distribution
\nu(\gamma)

(2)

F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\nu(F_{12})=1

(3)

F_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}

with the aim of showing
X_{2}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable with distribution
\nu(\gamma)
.
It suffices to show that
X_{2}
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable, as then it follows that

X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are almost-surely equal random variables with respect to
\mu

and so have the same distribution.
Fix arbitrary
A\in\mathcal{A}
with the aim of showing
X_{2}^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
.
Write
X_{2}^{-1}(A)
as the disjoint union

(X_{2}^{-1}(A)\cap F_{12})\uplus(X_{2}^{-1}(A)\cap F_{12}^{c})
.
The first disjunct is equal to
X_{1}^{-1}(A)\cap F_{12}

because
F_{12}
is the event
X_{1}=X_{2}
; this is in
\mathcal{F}
because

X_{1}^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
by (1) and
F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
by (2).
The second disjunct is in
\mathcal{F}
by (3) and Lemma
B.9
.
Thus
X_{2}^{-1}(A)
is a union of events in
\mathcal{F}
as required.
∎

Lemma B.15 (congruence).

Let
(\mathbf{A},\mathcal{A})
and
(\mathbf{B},\mathcal{B})
be measurable spaces that support equality.
Let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be random expressions of type
\mathbf{A}
.
Let
F[X]
be a random expression of type
\mathbf{B}
with a free variable
X
of type
\mathbf{A}
.
The following entailment holds:

\frac{}{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(F[E_{1}],F[E_{2}])\land(E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2})\vdash F[E_{1}]\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}F[E_{2}]}\quad\textsc{lab=Congruence}

Proof.

Fix a configuration
(\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu))
.
Let
X_{i}=E_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
for
i\in\{1,2,3\}

and let
E_{12}
be the event
X_{1}=X_{2}
.
Let
F_{1}=F(\gamma)\circ(D,X_{1})

and
F_{2}=F(\gamma)\circ(D,X_{2})
,
and let
F_{12}
be the event
F_{1}=F_{2}
.
We have

•

F_{1}
and
F_{2}
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable

•

E_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(E_{12})=1

•

E_{12}\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A})\subseteq\mathcal{F}

The goal is to show
F_{12}\in\mathcal{F}
and
\mu(F_{12})=1
and

F_{12}\cup(F_{1},F_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B})\subseteq\mathcal{F}
.

•

F_{12}=(F_{1},F_{2})^{-1}(\Delta_{\mathbf{B}})\in\mathcal{F}
because
F_{1}
and

F_{2}
are
\mathcal{F}
-measurable and
\mathbf{B}
supports equality.

•

\mu(F_{12})\geq\mu(E_{12})=1
because
E_{12}\subseteq F_{12}
.

•

\mathcal{F}
contains
F_{12}
and the entire pullback
\sigma
-algebra

(F_{1},F_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B})
, and so contains

F_{12}\cup(F_{1},F_{2})^{-1}(\mathcal{B}\otimes\mathcal{B})
too.

∎

Lemma B.16 ((
\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}
) as derived notion).

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a measurable space that supports equality and
let
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
be expressions of type
A
.
Let
\bf(A\otimes A)_{\bot}
be the measurable space with underlying set

\bf(A\otimes A)\cup\{\bot\}
and
\sigma
-algebra generated by
measurable subsets of
\bf A\otimes A
and the singleton set
\{\bot\}
.
The following equivalence holds:

E_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}E_{2}\dashv\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(\mathrm{if}~E_{1}=E_{2}~\mathrm{then}~\bot~\mathrm{else}~(E_{1},E_{2}))\land(\Ex[\mathbf{1}[E_{1}=E_{2}]]=1)

Proof.

Fix configuration
\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu)
. Let
X_{i}=E_{i}(\gamma)\circ D
for
i\in\{1,2\}

and let
F
be the event
X_{1}=X_{2}
.
Let
Y
be the random variable

\mathrm{if}~X_{1}=X_{2}~\mathrm{then}~\bot~\mathrm{else}~(E_{1},E_{2})
.
The left-hand side asserts
F
has probability
1
and
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}

for all
A\in\bf A\otimes A
.
The right-hand side asserts
F
has probability
1
and
that
Y
is
\mathcal{F}
-measurable.
To show the equivalence of these two assertions, it suffices to show that
measurability of
Y
is equivalent to having
F\cup(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}

for all
A\in\bf A\otimes A
.
First suppose
Y
is measurable and fix arbitrary
A\in\bf A\otimes A
.
Then measurability of
Y
says

\displaystyle Y^{-1}(A)

\displaystyle=(F\cap Y^{-1}(A))\uplus(F^{c}\cap Y^{-1}(A))=(F\cap\bot^{-1}(A))\uplus(F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))

\displaystyle=(F\cap\emptyset)\uplus(F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))=F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}

as required.
Conversely suppose
\mathcal{F}
contains
F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
for all
A\in\bf A\otimes A

and fix arbitrary
A\in\bf(A\otimes A)_{\bot}
. Then

\displaystyle Y^{-1}(A)

\displaystyle=(F\cap Y^{-1}(A))\uplus(F^{c}\cap Y^{-1}(A))=(F\cap\bot^{-1}(A))\uplus(F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A))

\displaystyle=(F\cap[\bot\in A])\uplus(F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A\setminus\{\bot\}))

where
[\bot\in A]
is
\bf A
if
\bot\in A
and
\emptyset
otherwise.
There are two cases.
If
\bot\notin A
then
Y^{-1}(A)=F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
If
\bot\in A
then
Y^{-1}(A)=F\cup(F^{c}\cap(X_{1},X_{2})^{-1}(A\setminus\{\bot\}))
,
a finite union of elements in
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
∎

B.6.
Derived rules

Lemma B.17.

The following structural rules hold:

\frac{P\vdash Q}{\mathsf{wp}(M,X.P)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(M,X.Q)}\quad\textsc{lab=H-Consequence}\frac{}{F*\mathsf{wp}(M,X.Q)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(M,X.F*Q)}\quad\textsc{lab=H-Frame,right=$(X\notin F)$}\frac{}{\mathsf{wp}(M,X.P)\lor\mathsf{wp}(M,X.Q)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(M,X.P\lor Q)}\quad\textsc{lab=H-Disjunction}

Proof.

We show the proof of the frame rule; the others are standard.
Suppose (1)

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{F}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{M}\vDash F*\mathsf{wp}(M,X.Q)

for some
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{F}\vDash F
and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{M}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M,X.Q)
.
To show
\mathsf{wp}(M,X.F*Q)
, further suppose

\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}(\mathcal{P}_{F}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{M})\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}{\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket}
.
By associativity,
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}(\mathcal{P}_{F}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{M})=(\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{F})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{M}

so specializing (1) with
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}:=\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{F}

gives
(\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{F})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu^{\prime})

and
X
such that

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow M(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash Q
.
Since
X\notin F
,
F[\mathrm{weak}_{X}]=F

so
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}_{F}\vDash F

by lemma
B.8
.
And since the composition
(\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{F})\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
is defined,
the composition
\mathcal{P}_{F}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
must be as well, so

\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}_{F}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}^{\prime}\vDash F*Q
as desired.
∎

Lemma B.18.

The following
\mathsf{wp}
laws hold:

\frac{}{Q[e/X]\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{ret}~e,X.Q)}\frac{}{\mathsf{wp}(M,X.\mathsf{wp}(N,Y.Q))\vdash\mathsf{wp}\left((X\leftarrow M;N),Y.Q\right)}\\
\frac{}{(\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~X\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]-\!\!*~Q)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1],X\mathrm{:}A.Q)}\\
\frac{}{(\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~X\sim\operatorname{Ber}p-\!\!*~Q)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\operatorname{Ber}p,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)}\\
\frac{}{I(1,e)*(\forall i\mathrm{:}\mathbb{N}.~\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~\{I(i,X)\}~M~\{X^{\prime}.\,I(i+1,X^{\prime})\})\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{for}(n,e,M),X\mathrm{:}A.~I(n+1,X))}\\
\frac{}{\mathsf{wp}(M,X\mathrm{:}A.~\mathsf{wp}(N,Y\mathrm{:}A.~Q(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y)))\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~M~\mathrm{else}~N,X\mathrm{:}A.~Q)}

where
\mathrm{for}(n,e,f)
is defined by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{for}(n,e,f)}=\mathrm{loop}(1,e,f)\textrm{where }\mathrm{loop}(k,e,f)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{ret}~e,&k>n\\
v\leftarrow f(k,e);~\mathrm{loop}(k+1,v,f),&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

Proof.

•

Ret:
suppose
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash Q[e(\gamma)/X]
.
By lemma
B.8
this is equivalent to
\gamma,(D,e(\gamma)),\mathcal{P}\vDash Q
.
To show
\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{ret}~e,X.Q)
suppose

\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket
.
Choose
\mathcal{P}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{P}
and
\mu^{\prime}:=\mu
and
X(\omega):=e(\gamma)
.
Then

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow(\mathrm{ret}~e)(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),e(\gamma))\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
\gamma,(D,e(\Gamma)),\mathcal{P}\vDash Q
as desired.

•

Let:
suppose
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(M,X.\mathsf{wp}(N,Y.Q))
.
To show
\mathsf{wp}((X\leftarrow M;N),Y.Q)
suppose

\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket
.
By assumption, there exist
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{X}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu_{X})

and
X
such that

(12)

\displaystyle\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&x\leftarrow M(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),x)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{X};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}_{X}\vDash\mathsf{wp}(N,Y.Q)
.
Applying this assumption gives
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{Y}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu_{Y})

and
Y
with

(13)

\displaystyle\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{X};\\
&y\leftarrow N(\gamma)((D,X)(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),(D,X)(\omega),y)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{Y};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),(D,X)(\omega),Y(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

and
\gamma,(D,X,Y),\mathcal{P}_{Y}\vDash Q
.
Since
X\notin Q
, this implies
\gamma,(D,Y),\mathcal{P}_{Y}\vDash Q
by
B.8
, so
it only remains to show

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&y\leftarrow(X\leftarrow M;N)(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),y)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{Y};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),Y(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

Calculate:

\displaystyle\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&y\leftarrow(X\leftarrow M;N)(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&x\leftarrow M(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&y\leftarrow N(\gamma)(D(\omega),x);\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),y)\end{aligned}~\right)

\displaystyle=\left(~\begin{aligned} &(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta,x)\leftarrow\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&x\leftarrow M(\gamma)(D(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),x)\end{aligned}~\right)\\
&y\leftarrow N(\gamma)(\delta,x);\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta,y)\end{aligned}~\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{let-binding-step1}}}{{=}}\left(~\begin{aligned} &(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta,x)\leftarrow\\
&\hskip 10.00002pt\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{X};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)\\
&y\leftarrow N(\gamma)(\delta,x);\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(\delta_{\textrm{ext}},\delta,y)\end{aligned}~\right)

\displaystyle=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{X};\\
&y\leftarrow N(\gamma)(\delta,X(\omega));\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),y)\end{aligned}~\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ref{let-binding-step2}}}{{=}}\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu_{Y};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),Y(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

•

Uniform:
suppose (1)
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~X\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]-\!\!*~Q
.
To show
\mathsf{wp}(\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1],X\mathrm{:}A.Q)
, suppose

\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and
D_{\textrm{ext}}:\operatorname{RV}\llbracket\Delta_{\textrm{ext}}\rrbracket
.
Let
n
witness
(D_{\textrm{ext}},\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P})
’s finite footprint.
Write the Hilbert cube as
[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}\cong[0,1]^{n}\otimes[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Define
\mu^{\prime}
via this isomorphism as the product measure

\mu|_{[0,1]^{n}}\otimes\lambda
, where
\lambda
assigns
to each finite-dimensional box
\prod_{i=1}^{n}[a_{i},b_{i}]\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}

the measure
\prod_{i=1}^{n}|b_{i}-a_{i}|
and extends to a measure on the whole Hilbert cube
by the Carathéodory extension theorem.
Let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
be the restriction of
\mu^{\prime}
to measurable sets of the form

[0,1]^{n}\times F\times[0,1]^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Let
X
be the projection
\pi_{n+1}=(\dots,\omega_{n+1},\dots)\mapsto\omega_{n+1}
.
By construction, the composite
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}
is defined
and
\mathcal{P}_{\textrm{frame}}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu^{\prime})

and
X
is
\mathcal{P}_{n}
-measurable and uniformly distributed in
[0,1]
.
Therefore
\gamma,(D,X),\mathcal{P}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{n}\vDash Q
by (1), and it only remains to show

\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1];\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

Calculate:

\displaystyle\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu;\\
&v\leftarrow\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1];\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega_{1\cdots n}\leftarrow\mu|_{[0,1]^{n}};\\
&v\leftarrow\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1];\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega_{1\cdots n}),D(\omega_{1\cdots n}),v)\end{aligned}~\right)

\displaystyle=\left(~\begin{aligned} &(\omega_{1\cdots n},v)\leftarrow\mu|_{[0,1]^{n}}\otimes\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1];\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega_{1\cdots n}),D(\omega_{1\cdots n}),v)\end{aligned}~\right)=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega_{1\cdots n+1}\leftarrow\mu^{\prime}|_{[0,1]^{n+1}};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega_{1\cdots n}),D(\omega_{1\cdots n}),\omega_{n+1})\end{aligned}~\right)

\displaystyle=\left(~\begin{aligned} &\omega\leftarrow\mu^{\prime};\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(D_{\textrm{ext}}(\omega),D(\omega),X(\omega))\end{aligned}~\right)

•

Flip: analogous to Uniform.

•

For:
suppose (1)
\forall i\mathrm{:}\mathbb{N}.~\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}\operatorname{RV}A.~\{I(i,X)\}~M~\{X^{\prime}.\,I(i+1,X^{\prime})\}
.
We need to show

I(1,e)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{for}(n,e,M),X\mathrm{:}A.~I(n+1,X)).

We generalize, and show

I(n+1-k,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1-k,V,M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

for all
V
and all
0\leq k\leq n
by induction on
k
, from which this follows at
k=n
.

–

Case
k=0
:

\displaystyle\top

\displaystyle\vdash I(n+1,V)-\!\!*~I(n+1,V)

\displaystyle\vdash I(n+1,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{ret}~V,X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vdash I(n+1,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1,V,M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\vdash I(n+1-k,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1-k,V,M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

–

Case
k=j+1\leq n
:
backwards reasoning from the goal gives

\displaystyle I(n+1-(j+1),V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1-(j+1),V,M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\!\!\dashv I(n-j,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n-j,V,M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\!\!\dashv I(n-j,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}((V^{\prime}\leftarrow M[n-j/i,V/X];~\mathrm{loop}(n+1-j,V^{\prime},M)),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\!\!\dashv I(n-j,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(M[n-j/i,V/X],V^{\prime}.~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1-j,V^{\prime},M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime})))

Now
I(n-j,V)-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(M[n-j/i,V/X],V^{\prime}.~I(n-j+1,V^{\prime}))
by (1)
so it suffices to show

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}(M[n-j/i,V/X],V^{\prime}.~I(n+1-j,V^{\prime}))

\displaystyle-\!\!*~

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}(M[n-j/i,V/X],V^{\prime}.~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1-j,V^{\prime},M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))).

The outer
\mathsf{wp}
s are the same, so by the consequence rule it suffices to show

\displaystyle I(n+1-j,V^{\prime})-\!\!*~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{loop}(n+1-j,V^{\prime},M),X^{\prime}.~I(n+1,X^{\prime}))

for all
V^{\prime}
, which is exactly the induction hypothesis at
j
.

•

If: applying properties of Markov kernels and rules for Let and Ret,

\displaystyle\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~M~\mathrm{else}~N,Z.~Q(Z))

\displaystyle\dashv\mathsf{wp}\left(\left(~\begin{aligned} &X\leftarrow M;\\
&Y\leftarrow N;\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y)\end{aligned}~\right),Z.~Q(Z)\right)

\displaystyle\dashv\mathsf{wp}\left(M,X.~\mathsf{wp}\left(\left(~\begin{aligned} &Y\leftarrow N;\\
&\mathrm{ret}~(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y)\end{aligned}~\right),Z.~Q(Z)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\dashv\mathsf{wp}(M,X.~\mathsf{wp}(N,Y.~\mathsf{wp}(\mathrm{ret}~(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y),Z.~Q(Z))))

\displaystyle\dashv\mathsf{wp}(M,X.~\mathsf{wp}(N,Y.~Q(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y)))

as desired.

∎

Corollary B.19.

The following
\mathsf{wp}
laws hold:

\frac{}{Q[\llbracket M\rrbracket/X]\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~M\rrbracket,X.Q)}\quad\textsc{lab=W-Ret}\frac{}{\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket M\rrbracket,X.\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket N\rrbracket,Y.Q))\vdash\mathsf{wp}\left(\llbracket X\leftarrow M\hskip 1.00006pt;~N\rrbracket,Y.Q\right)}\quad\textsc{lab=W-Let}\frac{}{(\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~X\sim\operatorname{Unif}[0,1]-\!\!*~Q)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}unif }}\texttt{[0,1]}\rrbracket,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)}\quad\textsc{lab=W-Uniform}\frac{}{(\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~X\sim\operatorname{Ber}p-\!\!*~Q)\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~p\rrbracket,X\mathrm{:}A.Q)}\quad\textsc{lab=W-Flip}\frac{}{I(1,e)*(\forall i\mathrm{:}\mathbb{N}.~\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~\{I(i,X)\}~M~\{X^{\prime}.\,I(i+1,X^{\prime})\})\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}for}}(n,e,i~X.~M)\rrbracket,X\mathrm{:}A.~I(n+1,X))}\quad\textsc{lab=W-For}\\
\frac{}{\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket M\rrbracket,X\mathrm{:}A.~\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket N\rrbracket,Y\mathrm{:}A.~Q(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y)))\vdash\mathsf{wp}(\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}EMN\rrbracket,X\mathrm{:}A.~Q)}

Proof.

Unfold
\llbracket-\rrbracket
and apply lemma
B.18
. ∎

Lemma B.20.

The following proof rules hold:

\frac{P\vdash P^{\prime}\quad Q^{\prime}\vdash Q\quad\{P^{\prime}\}~M~\{X.\,Q^{\prime}\}}{\{P\}~M~\{X.\,Q\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-Consequence}\frac{\{P\}~M~\{X.\,Q\}}{\{F*P\}~M~\{X.\,F*Q\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-Frame $(X\notin F)$}\frac{\{P\}~M~\{X.\,Q\}\quad\{P^{\prime}\}~M~\{X.\,Q^{\prime}\}}{\{P\lor P^{\prime}\}~M~\{X.\,Q\lor Q^{\prime}\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-Disjunction}\frac{~}{\{Q\big[\llbracket M\rrbracket/X\big]\}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~M~\{X.\,Q\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-Ret}\frac{\{P\}~M~\{X.\,Q\}\quad\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X.~\{Q\}~N~\{Y.\,R\}}{\{P\}~X\leftarrow M\hskip 1.00006pt;~N~\{Y.\,R\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-Let}\frac{~}{\{\top\}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}unif }}\texttt{[0,1]}~\{X.\,X\sim\operatorname{Unif}~[0,1]\})}\quad\textsc{right=H-Uniform}\frac{~}{\{\top\}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~p~\{X.\,X\sim\operatorname{Ber}p\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-Flip}\frac{\forall i\mathrm{:}\mathbb{N}.~\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X\mathrm{:}A.~\{I(i,X)\}~M~\{X^{\prime}.\,I(i+1,X^{\prime})\}}{\{I(1,e)\}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}for}}(n,e,i~X.~M)~\{X\mathrm{:}A.\,I(n+1,X)\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-For}\frac{\{P\}~M~\{X.\,Q(X)\}\quad\quad\forall_{\mathsf{rv}}X.~\{Q(X)\}~N~\{Y.\,R(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~X~\mathrm{else}~Y)\}}{\{P\}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~E~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~M~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~N~\{Z.\,R(Z)\}}\quad\textsc{right=H-If}

Proof.

Rules
H-Consequence
,
H-Frame
, and
H-Disjunction

follow from Lemma
B.17
;
the remaining rules follow from Corollary
B.19
.
All proofs go by unfolding the definition of the Hoare triple
and applying the relevant
\mathsf{wp}
law.
∎

B.7.
Disintegration

Lemma B.21.

Let
(M,\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}},\sqsubseteq,1)
be a KRM with
1\sqsubseteq x
for all
x
.
Let
A
be a downward-closed subset of
M
(i.e.,
x\sqsubseteq y\in A
implies
x\in A
).
Let
(\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}^{\prime})
be the restriction of
(\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}})
to
A
; that is,

\displaystyle x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}y:=\begin{cases}x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}y,&x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}y\in A\\
\mathrm{undefined},&\textrm{otherwise}\end{cases}

Then
(A,\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}^{\prime},\sqsubseteq,1)
is a KRM.

Proof.

Unit and commutativity are straightforward.
For associativity, note that if
1\sqsubseteq x
for all
x

then by monotonicity of
(\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}})
we have
x\sqsubseteq x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}y
for all
x,y
.
Now suppose
x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}y
defined and
(x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}y)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}z
defined.
Then
(x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}y)\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}z\in A
and by downward closure so is
y\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}z

and by associativity so is
x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}(y\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}z)
, so both
y\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}z
and
x\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}(y\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\!^{\prime}z)

are defined and associativity is inherited from associativity of
(\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}})
.
∎

Theorem B.22.

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{disintegrable}}
be the set of countably-generated probability spaces
\mathcal{P}
that have finite footprint
and can be extended to a Borel measure on the entire Hilbert cube.
The restriction of the KRM given by Theorem
2.2
to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{disintegrable}}
is still a KRM.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.5
,
the restriction to
\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{finite}}
is a KRM, so it suffices to show that restricting to
countably-generated spaces that can be extended to a Borel measure still yields a KRM.
First, restricting to countably-generated spaces still yields a KRM because
the independent combination of two countably-generated spaces remains couuntably-generated.
Then, the restriction to spaces that can be extended to a Borel measure
still yields a KRM by Lemma
B.21
,
as the set of spaces that can be extended to a Borel measure is downward-closed.
∎

\frac{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash_{{\textrm{rv}}}E:A\quad\Gamma,x\mathrm{:}A;\Delta\vdash P}{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash\D_{x\mathrm{:}A\leftarrow E}P}

\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\D_{x\mathrm{:}A\leftarrow E}P

iff

for all
(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)\sqsupseteq\mathcal{P}

and all
(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)
-disintegrations

\{\nu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
with respect to
E(\gamma)\circ D

and
(E\circ D)_{*}\mu
-almost-all
x\in A
,

(\gamma,x),D,\nu_{x}|_{\mathcal{P}}\vDash P

Lemma B.23.

If
\mu
and
\nu
are probability measures on a space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})

generated by a
\pi
-system
\mathcal{B}
, then

\mu=\nu
iff
\mu(B)=\nu(B)
for all
B\in\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

The left-to-right direction is straightforward.
The right-to-left direction follows from the
\pi
-
\lambda
theorem:
the set
S:=\{B\in\mathcal{B}\mid\mu(B)=\nu(B)\}

is a
\lambda
-system
and
\mu
and
\nu
agree on a
\pi
-system that generates
\mathcal{F}
by assumption.
∎

Lemma B.24.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E*P\vdash\D_{x\mathrm{:}A\leftarrow E}P
.

Proof.

Suppose
\gamma,D,\underbrace{\mathcal{P}_{E}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{P}}_{\mathcal{P}}\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E*P

and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{E}\vDash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E

and (1)
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{P}\vDash P
.
To show
\D_{x\leftarrow E}P
, suppose

\mathcal{P}_{E}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{P}\sqsubseteq(\Sigma_{\Omega},\mu)

and let
\{\nu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be a
\mu
-disintegration with respect to
E(\gamma)\circ D
.
We need to show
(\gamma,x),D,\nu_{x}|_{\mathcal{P}}\vDash P
for almost all
x\in A
.
Since
x\notin P
,
(\gamma,x),D,\nu_{x}|_{\mathcal{P}}\vDash P
is equivalent to

\gamma,D,\nu_{x}|_{\mathcal{P}}\vDash P
,
so by
assumption (1) and monotonicity it suffices to show
\nu_{x}|_{\mathcal{P}_{P}}=\mathcal{P}_{P}
for almost all
x\in A
.

Write
\mathcal{P}_{P}=(\mathcal{F}_{P},\mu_{P})
and
let
S:=\{x\in A\mid\nu_{x}|_{\mathcal{F}_{P}}=\mu_{P}\}
.
It’s enough to show that
S
is
\Sigma_{A}
-measurable and has probability
1
.
Let
\mathcal{B}=\{B_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a countable basis of
\mathcal{F}_{P}
; without loss of generality we may
assume
\mathcal{B}
is a
\pi
-system because any countable collection of sets has countable closure
under finite intersections.
By lemma
B.23
, we can write
S
as the countable intersection

S=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}S_{n}
where
S_{n}:=\{x\in A\mid\nu_{x}(B_{n})=\mu_{P}(B_{n})\}
.
Because
\sigma
-algebras are closed under countable intersections and measures are countably
subadditive,
S
is measurable with probability
1
if each
S_{n}
is.

Each
S_{n}
is
\Sigma_{A}
-measurable:

S_{n}
is equal to the preimage of the singleton set
\{\mu_{P}(B_{n})\}

under the map
\nu_{(-)}(B_{n})
; since
\nu
is a Markov kernel and singletons are Borel,
this preimage must be

\Sigma_{A}
-measurable.
It only remains to show each
S_{n}
has probability
1
.
Now, suppose for the sake of contradiction that there is some
k
such that
S_{k}
does not have probability
1
,
so
\nu_{x}(B_{n})\neq\mu_{P}(B_{n})
for all
x\in N:=A\setminus S_{k}
.
We can write
N
as a disjoint union of two subsets
N_{<}
and
N_{>}
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle N_{<}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in N\mid\nu_{x}(B_{n})<\mu_{P}(B_{n})\}

\displaystyle N_{>}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in N\mid\nu_{x}(B_{n})>\mu_{P}(B_{n})\}

These are both measurable, since they can be written as preimages of
[0,\mu_{P}(B_{n}))

and
(\mu_{P}(B_{n}),1]
under
\nu_{(-)}(B_{n})
.
Because
N
has nonzero probability, at least one of
N_{<}
or
N_{>}
must have nonzero probability too.
Suppose it’s
N_{<}
; the case where
N_{>}
has nonzero probability is analogous.
Because
\nu
is a disintegration of
\mu
with respect to
E(\gamma)\circ D
, we have

\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}f(\omega)=\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}f(\omega)

for all
f:\Omega\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathrm{m}\,}}}{{\to}}\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Choose
f(\omega):=\mathbf{1}[\omega\in B_{n}]\mathbf{1}[E(\gamma)(D(\omega))\in N_{<}]
.
Then simplifying LHS gives

\displaystyle\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}f(\omega)

\displaystyle=\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}\mathbf{1}[\omega\in B_{n}]\mathbf{1}[E(\gamma)(D(\omega))\in N_{<}]

\displaystyle\hskip-3.00003pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}\mathbf{1}[\omega\in B_{n}]\Ex_{\omega\sim\mu}\mathbf{1}[E(\gamma)(D(\omega))\in N_{<}]

\displaystyle=\mu(B_{n})(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu(N_{<})

Step (a) uses independence of
B_{n}
and
(E(\gamma)\circ D)^{-1}(N_{<})
:

\mathcal{P}_{E}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}_{P}
defined and
B_{n}\in\mathcal{P}_{P}
and
(E(\gamma)\circ D)^{-1}(N_{<})\in\mathcal{P}_{E}
.
Meanwhile, simplifying RHS gives

\displaystyle\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}f(\omega)

\displaystyle=\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}\mathbf{1}[\omega\in B_{n}]\mathbf{1}[E(\gamma)(D(\omega))\in N_{<}]

\displaystyle\hskip-3.00003pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}\mathbf{1}[\omega\in B_{n}]\mathbf{1}[x\in N_{<}]

\displaystyle=\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\mathbf{1}[x\in N_{<}]\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}\mathbf{1}[\omega\in B_{n}]

\displaystyle=\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\mathbf{1}[x\in N_{<}]\nu_{x}(B_{n})

\displaystyle\hskip-3.00003pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{<}}\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\mathbf{1}[x\in N_{<}]\mu_{P}(B_{n})

\displaystyle=\mu_{P}(B_{n})\Ex_{x\sim(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu}\mathbf{1}[x\in N_{<}]

\displaystyle=\mu_{P}(B_{n})(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu(N_{<})

\displaystyle=\mu(B_{n})(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\mu(N_{<})

Step (a) holds because
(E(\gamma)\circ D)_{*}\nu_{x}(\{x\})=1
for almost all
x
.
Step (b) holds because the expectation is taken over
x\in N_{<}
,
where the inequality holds by assumption; the inequality remains strict because
N_{<}
is
nonnegligible.
Putting these two together gives
\mathrm{LHS}=\mathrm{RHS}
and
\mathrm{LHS}<\mathrm{RHS}
, a
contradiction.
∎

Lemma B.25 (law of total expectation).

The following entailment holds:

\Ex[e[X/x]]=v~~\land~~\D_{x:A\leftarrow X}\Ex[E]=e~~~\vdash~~~\Ex[E]=v

Proof.

Fix
(\gamma,D,\mathcal{P})
.
By the first conjunct
\Ex_{\omega\sim\mathcal{P}}e(\gamma,X(\gamma)(D(\omega)))=v(\gamma)
.
By assumption,
\mathcal{P}
extends to a Borel measure
\mu
on the Hilbert cube.
By the disintegration theorem, there exists at least one
\mu
-disintegration
with respect to
X(\gamma)\circ D
; call it
\{\nu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
.
By the second conjunct
\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}[E(\gamma)(D(\omega))]=e(\gamma,x)
for almost all
x\in A
.
This along with the existence of the disintegration
\nu
implies

\displaystyle\Ex_{\omega\sim\mathcal{P}}E(\gamma)(D(\omega))=\Ex_{x\sim{(X(\gamma)\circ D)}_{*}\mu}\Ex_{\omega\sim\nu_{x}}E(\gamma)(D(\omega))=\Ex_{x\sim{(X(\gamma)\circ D)}_{*}\mu}e(\gamma,x)=\Ex_{\omega\in\mu}e(\gamma,X(\gamma)(D(\omega)))=v(\gamma)

as desired.
∎

Lemma B.26.

The following entailments hold:
