\frac{P~\vdash~Q}{\D_{x\leftarrow E}P~\vdash~\D_{x\leftarrow E}Q}\quad\textsc{lab=C-Entail}\frac{}{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E*P~\vdash~\D_{x\leftarrow E}P}\quad\textsc{lab=C-Indep}\frac{}{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}\big(X\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}x\big)}\quad\textsc{lab=C-Subst}\frac{}{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E}\quad\textsc{lab=C-Own}\frac{}{\D_{x\leftarrow X}\Ex[E]=e\hskip 5.0pt\land\hskip 5.0pt\Ex[e[X/x]]=v\hskip 7.5pt\vdash\hskip 7.5pt\Ex[E]=v}\quad\textsc{lab=C-Total-Expectation}

Proof.

C-Indep
and
C-Total-Expectation
follow from lemmas

B.24
and
B.25
respectively.

•

C-Entail
: suppose
P\vdash Q
and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\D_{x\mathrm{:}A\leftarrow E}P
.
Let
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be a disintegration of
\mathcal{P}
with respect to
E(\gamma)\circ D
;
let
\{\mathcal{P}_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be the corresponding restrictions of
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
to
\mathcal{P}
.
By assumption,
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{x}\vDash P
for almost-all
x\in A
.
Since
P\vdash Q
, this implies
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{x}\vDash Q
for almost-all
x\in A
as desired.

•

C-Subst
:
Fix
(\gamma,D,\mathcal{P})
.
Let
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be a disintegration of
\mathcal{P}
with respect to
X(\gamma)\circ D
.
Let
E_{x}
be the event that
X(\gamma)\circ D
is equal to
x
.
By assumption we have that
X
is
\mathcal{P}
-measurable, so it only remains to show
that
E_{x}
holds almost-surely with respect to
\mu_{x}
for almost all
x\in A
.
By the definition of disintegration,
the event
\{\omega\mid X(\omega)=x\}
has probability
1
under
\mu_{x}

for almost all
x
as required.

•

C-Own
:
Fix
(\gamma,D,(\mathcal{F},\mu))
and
let
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be a disintegration of
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
with respect to

X(\gamma)\circ D
. Disintegration only changes the probability measure;
the underlying
\sigma
-algebra remains fixed. Thus if
E(\gamma)\circ D
is

\mathcal{F}
-measurable then it remains
\mathcal{F}
-measurable under each
conditional probability space.

∎

Lemma B.27.

The following entailments hold:

•

Necessitation: if
\vdash P
then
\displaystyle\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}P
.

•

Distribution:
\displaystyle\D_{x\leftarrow X}(P\to Q)\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}P\to\D_{x\leftarrow X}Q
.

•

Distributes over
(\land)
:
\displaystyle\D_{x\leftarrow X}(P\land Q)\dashv\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}P\land\D_{x\leftarrow X}Q
.

•

Semidistributes over
(\lor)
:
\displaystyle\D_{x\leftarrow X}P\lor\D_{x\leftarrow X}Q\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}(P\lor Q)
.

Proof.

•

Necessitation: if
P
holds in all configurations
then it holds for all disintegrated configurations as well.

•

Distribution: it suffices to show
D_{x\leftarrow X}(P\to Q)\land\D_{x\leftarrow X}P\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}Q
.
By
C-And
the premise is equivalent to
\D_{x\leftarrow X}((P\to Q)\land P)
;
the result then follows from
C-Entail

via the entailment
(P\to Q)\land P\vdash Q
.

•

Distributes over
(\land
):
the left-to-right direction follows from
C-Entail

via the entailments
P\land Q\vdash P
and
P\land Q\vdash Q
.
For the right-to-left entailment, suppose
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\D_{x\leftarrow E}P

and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}\vDash\D_{x\leftarrow E}Q

and let
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be a disintegration of
\mathcal{P}
with respect to
E(\gamma)\circ D
;
let
\{\mathcal{P}_{x}\}_{x\in A}
be the corresponding restrictions of
\{\mu_{x}\}_{x\in A}
to
\mathcal{P}
.
By assumption, there are two sets
F_{1},F_{2}\subseteq A
of measure
1

such that
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{x}\vDash P
for all
x\in F_{1}

and
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{x}\vDash Q
for all
x\in F_{2}
.
Therefore,
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{x}\vDash P\land Q
for all
x\in F_{1}\cap F_{2}
.
Moreover,
F_{1}\cap F_{2}
has measure
1
by subadditivity,
so
\gamma,D,\mathcal{P}_{x}\vDash P\land Q
for almost-all
x
as desired.

•

Semidistributes over
(\lor)
:
it suffices to show
\D_{x\leftarrow X}P\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}(P\lor Q)

and
\D_{x\leftarrow X}Q\vdash\D_{x\leftarrow X}(P\lor Q)
.
These follow from
C-Entail
via the entailments

P\vdash P\lor Q
and
Q\vdash P\lor Q
respectively.

∎

program

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\top\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptZ\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~Z~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 18.49988ptX_{1}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~p\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{X_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p\right\}}

else

\displaystyle\hskip 18.49988pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}~X_{1}~Y_{1}.~~X_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p~~*~~Y_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 18.49988pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(Z,X_{2},Y_{2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &Z\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}~X_{1}~Y_{1}~X_{2}~Y_{2}.~~X_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p~~*~~Y_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p~~*~~X_{2}\sim\operatorname{Ber}q~~*~~Y_{2}\sim\operatorname{Ber}q~~*~~\\
&X\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~Z~\mathrm{then}~X_{1}~\mathrm{else}~X_{2})~~*~~Y\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~Z~\mathrm{then}~Y_{1}~\mathrm{else}~Y_{2})\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\D_{z\leftarrow Z}\underbrace{\left(\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}~X_{1}~Y_{1}~X_{2}~Y_{2}.~~\begin{aligned} &X_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p~~*~~Y_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}p~~*~~X_{2}\sim\operatorname{Ber}q~~*~~Y_{2}\sim\operatorname{Ber}q~~*~~\\

Appendix E
Proving a Weighted Sampling Algorithm Correct (Full)

\displaystyle 1~\,W\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(w_{1}+\dots+w_{n})\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle 2~\,(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(w_{1}/W,\dots,w_{n}/W)\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle 3~\,U\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}unif }}\texttt{[0,1]}\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle 4~\,\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}for}}(n,0,i~J.~

\displaystyle 7~\,\hskip 10.00002pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~J)

(a)

Naive linear-space implementation of weighted sampling.

\displaystyle 1~\,w\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~[w_{1},\dots,w_{n}]\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle 2~\,M\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(-\infty)\hskip 1.00006pt;K\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(0)\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle 3~\,\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}for}}(n,(M,K),i~(M,K).~

\displaystyle 4~\,\hskip 10.00002ptS\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}unif }}\texttt{[0,1]}\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle 5~\,\hskip 10.00002ptU\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(S\,^\,(1\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}/}}w[i]))\hskip 1.00006pt;

Constant-space version.

.

Figure 11
.
Weighted sampling example. The constants
w_{i}
are inputs.

To exercise Lilac’s support for conditional reasoning, continuous random variables,
and substructural handling of independence, we now prove a sophisticated
constant-space
weighted sampling algorithm
correct using Lilac. Suppose
you are given a collection of items
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
each with associated
weight
w_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. The task is to draw a sample from the collection

\{x_{i}\}
in a manner where each item is drawn with probability proportional to
its weight. This problem is an instance of
reservoir
sampling

(
Efraimidis and Spirakis 2006
)
, and is an important primitive in
distributed systems.

First, we consider a naive solution
that requires space linear in the number of weights; pseudocode
for this algorithm is presented in Figure
11(a)
. The first
pass over the weights occurs on Line 1, which computes the normalizing constant

W=\sum_{i}w_{i}
. Line 2 then divides each weight by
W
so that the result

(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
forms a probability distribution.
This distribution can be thought of as a partitioning of the interval
[0,1]

into
n
subintervals
with lengths
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
; to sample from it
we can choose a point
U
uniformly at random from
[0,1]
(Line 3)
and select the item corresponding to the subinterval that
U
lands in (Lines 4–7).

While simple to understand and implement, this naive approach has a critical
flaw that makes it inappropriate for application in large-scale systems: it requires storing
all previously encountered weights and scanning over them before a single sample can be
drawn, and so does not scale to a streaming setting where new weights are acquired
one at a time (for instance, as each user visits a website). To fix this
limitation,
Efraimidis and Spirakis 2006
proposed the very clever

constant-space solution
presented in Figure
11(b)
. The
core of this approach is to generate a value
S
uniformly at random from
[0,1]
on
every

iteration (Line 3), perturb
S
according to the next weight
w_{i}
in the stream
(Line 4), and track only the
greatest
perturbed sample (Lines 5–8). Figure
11(c)

gives some intuition for the perturbed quantity
S^{1/w_{i}}
on Line 4: if
w_{i}

is large (i.e., item
i
has high weight), then
S^{1/w_{i}}
is likely to be large (visualized
by the curve
w=4
); if
w_{i}
is small, then
S^{1/w_{i}}
is likely to be small (visualized
by the curve
w=1/4
).
The fact that this program is equivalent to the naive one is quite surprising,
and proving it requires the simultaneous application of several important theorems from
probability theory. We show how this can be done formally in Lilac
in a manner similar to a typical informal proof.
Correctness is captured by the following Lilac postcondition:

(14)

\displaystyle{\forall k.\Pr(K=k)=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}

To establish this postcondition, a typical informal proof
begins by declaring mutually independent, uniformly distributed random variables
\{S_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
,
where
S_{i}
denotes the value sampled by Line 4 on the
i
th loop iteration,
and a random variable
K=\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}
that denotes the final result.
Implicit in this setup are the assumptions that each
S_{i}
produced by the program is actually
independent and uniformly distributed, and that the for-loop actually computes
the specified
\argmax
. We can formally establish this
by mechanically applying the proof rules described in Section
2.5

to conclude the following at program termination:

(15)

\displaystyle{\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}S_{1}\dots S_{n}.~\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]~~*~~K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}

The proof makes use of the following
invariant
I_{j}
for the loop on
Line 2, which must hold immediately before the execution of the
j
th iteration for all
1\leq j\leq n+1
:

(16)

\displaystyle I_{j}\hskip 7.5pt:=\hskip 7.5pt{\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}S_{1}\dots S_{j}.~~\hugestar_{1\leq i<j}~S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\hskip 5.0pt*\hskip 5.0ptK\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{1\leq i<j}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}\hskip 5.0pt*\hskip 5.0ptM\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\max_{1\leq i<j}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}

The proof that our program maintains this invariant is completely standard for separation logics,
so we elide the details and focus on the challenge of

).
To show (
14
) in the case

,

\displaystyle\Pr(K=k)=\Pr\left(S_{k}^{1/w_{k}}>S_{j}^{1/w_{j}}\text{ for all }j\neq k\right),

since

j

This is an unwieldy probability to compute directly. The trick is to use conditioning:
in this case, fixing
S_{k}

(18)

\displaystyle\Pr(K=k\mid S_{k}=s_{k})

\displaystyle=\Pr\left(s_{k}^{1/w_{k}}>S_{j}^{1/w_{j}}\text{ for all }j\neq k\right)

(19)

\displaystyle=\Pr\left(s_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}\text{ for all }j\neq k\right)

Exponentiating

(20)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\neq k}\Pr\left(s_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}\right)

By conditional independence

(21)

\displaystyle=\prod_{j\neq k}s_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}

\displaystyle S_{j}\text{ uniform}

(22)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{pow}\left(s_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}\right).

Formally, this calculation occurs under the modality
\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}
, which is introduced via
C-Indep
.
The expression
\Pr(E)
abbreviates
\Ex[\mathbf{1}[E]]
, the expectation of the indicator random variable
\mathbf{1}[E]
.
17
17

17

If
E
is an event then the random variable
\mathbf{1}[E]
is
1
if
E

0
otherwise.

A critical step occurs in Equation
20
: each

S_{j}
is conditionally independent from all others given
S_{k}=s_{k}
. This
permits a critical simplification: the probability of the conjunction becomes a
product of simpler probabilities. This is an application of the derived rule

(
Indep-Prod
)

\displaystyle{\hugestar_{i}\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}E_{i}~~~\vdash~~~\Pr\Big(\bigcap_{i}E_{i}\Big)=\prod_{i}\Pr(E_{i}),}

an immediate consequence of Lemma
2.3
.
Note that our modal treatment of conditioning leads to a nice separation of concerns here.
Because
\D

that appear to be

probability are automatically lifted to facts like Equation (
20
),
with the expected conditional reading.

Finally, to complete the proof we connect the conditional

\Pr(K=k\mid S_{k}=s_{k})

\Pr(K=k)

using the law of total expectation:

(23)

\displaystyle\Pr(K=k)

\displaystyle=\Ex\left[\Pr(K=k\mid S_{k})\right]

Law of Total Expectation

(24)

\displaystyle=\Ex\left[\operatorname{pow}\left(S_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\text{By }(\ref{eq:eqset1:end-full})

(25)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}+1\right)^{-1}

S_{k}
uniform

(26)

\displaystyle=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}.

Unlike the calculation in Equations

–

The gap between the two calculations is bridged by

\displaystyle\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\Big(\Ex[\underbrace{\mathbf{1}[K=k]}_{E}]=\underbrace{\operatorname{pow}\left(s_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}{w_{j}}}{w_{k}}\right)}_{e}\Big)\land\Big(\Ex\Big[\underbrace{\mathrm{pow}\Big(S_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}\Big)}_{e[S_{k}/s_{k}]}\Big]=\underbrace{\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}_{v}\Big)~~{\vdash}~~\Ex[\underbrace{\mathbf{1}[K=k]}_{E}]=\underbrace{\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}_{v}

Putting all this together yields a formal proof of correctness in Lilac.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\top\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{W\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})~~*~~M\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}-\infty\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{W\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})~~*~~M\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}-\infty~~*~~K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}0\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\text{Let }\begin{aligned} I(i,M,K)\hskip 9.24994pt:=\hskip 9.24994pt\left(\begin{aligned} &W\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(w_{1},\dots,w_{n})~~*~~1\leq i\leq n~~*~~\\

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{I(1,M,K)\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}for}}(n,(M,K),i~(M,K).~

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{I(i,M,K)\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\hskip 9.24994ptS\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}unif }}\texttt{[0,1]}\hskip 0.92505pt;

&M^{\prime}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\max(U,M)~~*~~U^{\prime}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\mathrm{if}~U>M~\mathrm{then}~i~\mathrm{else}~K\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{(M^{\prime},K^{\prime}).~~I(i+1,M^{\prime},K^{\prime})\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\exists_{\mathsf{rv}}S_{1}\dots S_{n}.~\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]~~*~~K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}\right\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\forall k.\Pr(K=k)=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}\right\}}

To illustrate the proof of the final entailment,
we animate the proof state at each step in inference-rule notation,
in the style of interactive theorem provers such as Coq.
First we work backwards from the goal:

\frac{\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Pr[K=k]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Pr[\forall j\neq k.~S_{k}^{1/w_{k}}>S_{j}^{1/w_{j}}]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Pr[\forall j\neq k.~S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\mathbf{1}[\forall j\neq k.~S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{\hugestar_{i}S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}

At this point we begin working forwards from the hypotheses,
using
C-Indep

to introduce the conditioning modality
\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}

with the aim of computing the conditional probability
\Pr(K=k\mid S_{k}=s_{k})
.

\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\hugestar_{j\neq k}S_{j}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\hugestar_{j\neq k}S_{j}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\left(\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[s_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]\right)\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}

Next, we use conditional independence of
\{S_{j}\}_{j\neq k}
given
S_{k}
, encoded in the
iterated separating conjunction underneath
\D_{s_{\leftarrow}S_{k}}
, to interchange product and expectation:

\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\hugestar_{j\neq k}S_{j}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\left(\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\prod_{j\neq k}\Ex[\mathbf{1}[s_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]]\right)\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}

Now significant simplifications are possible, completing the first calculation
(Equations
18
–
22
):

\frac{S_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\hugestar_{j\neq k}S_{j}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\left(\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\prod_{j\neq k}s_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}\right)\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\hugestar_{j\neq k}S_{j}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\left(\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\exp\left(s_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}\right)\right)\quad K\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\argmax_{i}S_{i}^{1/w_{i}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\D_{s_{k}\leftarrow S_{k}}\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\exp\left(s_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}\right)}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}

Having completed the computation of the conditional probability
\Pr(K=k\mid S_{k}=s_{k})

by working forwards from the hypotheses, we eliminate the conditioning modality by applying the
law of total expectation (
C-Total-Expectation
):

\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\Ex\left[\exp\left(S_{k},\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}\right)\right]}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}

The remainder of the calculation is straightforward, following
Equations
23
–
26
:

\frac{S_{k}\sim\mathrm{Unif}~[0,1]\land\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{1}{\frac{\sum_{j\neq k}w_{j}}{w_{k}}+1}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}{\Ex\left[\prod_{j\neq k}\mathbf{1}[S_{k}^{w_{j}/w_{k}}>S_{j}]\right]=\frac{w_{k}}{\sum_{j}w_{j}}}\\
\frac{}{\mathbf{QED}}

Appendix F
Examples from Barthe et. al.

In this section we consider three of the five examples presented in
Barthe et al. 2019
:
one-time pad, oblivious transfer, and private information retrieval.
In each example, the goal is to
verify the perfect secrecy of a cryptographic protocol. Perfect secrecy is established
via two methods: uniformity, which aims to show that each agent’s view of others’ data is
uniformly distributed at exit, and input independence, which aims to show that the
encrypted output of the protocol is independent of the input.

Barthe et al. 2019
use PSL to establish perfect secrecy of one-time pad and
private information retrieval via both uniformity and input independence,
and perfect secrecy of oblivious transfer via uniformity.
We will show how the same can be done in Lilac.

Barthe et al. 2019
also observe that
the input independence proof for oblivious transfer gets stuck, mentioning that
even an informal proof sketch does not seem easy.
We will show that the
postcondition specifying input indendence for oblivious transfer is in fact unsatisfiable
by giving a countermodel.

To do this, we add some support for length-
n
bitvectors and reasoning about uniformity.
For bitvectors,

•

Let
\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2
be the uniform distribution on boolean-valued
n
-tuples.

•

Let
\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~p
be the
n
-ary generalization of
flip
that produces
n
-tuples of i.i.d.
\operatorname{Ber}p

random variables, with the evident semantics.

•

If
X
is a random variable valued in boolean
n
-tuples, let
\bigoplus X

be the random variable given by the
\oplus
of all
n
components.

•

Let
\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}
and
\oplus^{n}
be the lifting of boolean
&&
and
\oplus
to
n
-tuples.

•

We will make use of algebraic properties of the bitvector xor operator
\oplus^{n}
throughout;
in particular the property that
x\oplus^{n}-
is invertible.

Next, we import the requisite probability theory facts as derived rules.
For clarity of exposition, we suppress components of Lilac’s semantic model
(like underlying probability spaces, the random substitution, and the deterministic substitution)
in the proofs of these rules in favor of a presentation that more closely mirrors textbook probability.
The first few facts concern uniformity of random bitvectors:

Lemma F.1.

If
X
is a random
n
-bitvector and
f:\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}bool}}\rrbracket^{n}\to\llbracket\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}bool}}\rrbracket^{n}
a bijection then

X\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\vdash f(X)\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2.

Proof.

We have
\Pr[f(X)=x]=\Pr[X=f^{-1}(x)]=1/2^{n}
for all
x
.
∎

Lemma F.2.

If
X
is a random
n
-bitvector and
Y
a random
m
-bitvector then

X\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Y\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{m}1/2\dashv\vdash(X,Y)\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{m+n}1/2

Proof.

Calculation gives

\Pr[(X,Y)=(x,y)]=\Pr[X=x,Y=y]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\Pr[X=x]\Pr[Y=y]=(1/2^{m})(1/2^{n})=1/2^{m+n}

for all
x,y
as desired. Equation
(a)
follows from independence of
X
and
Y
.
∎

This next lemma encodes the key fact of probability theory underlying the perfect secrecy of the examples
we will consider in the next section. Intuitively, it states that any random variable which is “conditionally
uniformly distributed” (that is, uniformly distributed conditional on some other random variable) is
uniformly distributed proper.

Lemma F.3.

If
X
is a random variable taking on finitely many values
19
19

19

Though we expect this restriction can be lifted,
\operatorname{cod}(X)
finite suffices for our examples.

and
Y
a random
n
-bitvector then

\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X\land\D_{x\leftarrow X}(Y\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\hskip 5.0pt\vdash\hskip 5.0pt\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X~~*~~Y\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2

Proof.

It suffices to show
X
and
Y
are independent and
Y
uniform.
This amounts to showing the equality
\Pr[X=x,Y=y]=\Pr[X=x]/2^{n}
for all
x,y
.
By assumption
Y
is uniformly distributed conditional on
X
, so

\Pr[X=x,Y=y]=\Pr[Y=y\mid X=x]\Pr[X=x]=\frac{1}{2^{n}}\Pr[X=x]

as desired.
∎

To avoid verbosity, we use the abbreviation

\displaystyle\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{x\leftarrow X}P:=\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X\land\D_{x\leftarrow X}P
.

Thus the entailment given by Lemma
F.3
can be written

\displaystyle\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{x\leftarrow X}(Y\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\hskip 5.0pt\vdash\hskip 5.0pt\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X~~*~~Y\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2
.

We will also frequently make use of the following variant of
C-Indep
:

Lemma F.4.

The following entailment holds:

\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X~~*~~P\hskip 5.0pt\vdash\hskip 5.0pt\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{x\leftarrow X}P.

Proof.

By the following chain:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X*P

\displaystyle\vdash(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X*P)\land(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X*P)

\displaystyle\text{idempotency of }\land

\displaystyle\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X\land(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X*P)

drop a conjunct

\displaystyle\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X\land\D_{x\leftarrow X}P

C-Indep

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{x\leftarrow X}P

\displaystyle\text{definition of }\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}

∎

The final derived rule we will make use of encodes the probability-theoretic fact that,
when proving an assertion of the form
X\sim\mu~~*~~P
,
one can first establish that
X
has distribution
\mu
,
and then separately establish independence of
X
from other random variables.

Lemma F.5.

Let
X
be a random variable,
\mu
a distribution, and
P
a proposition.

(X\sim\mu)\land(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X~~*~~P)\vdash(X\sim\mu)~~*~~P

Proof.

Suppose
X
is distributed as
\mu
with respect to probability space
\mathcal{P}_{X}
,
that
P
holds in space
\mathcal{P}
,
and that there exists a space
\mathcal{P}_{X}^{\prime}
independent of
\mathcal{P}
for which
X
is
\mathcal{P}_{X}^{\prime}
-measurable.
Let
\mathcal{Q}
be the pullback
\sigma
-algebra of
X
.
We have that
\mathcal{P}_{X}\sqsupseteq\mathcal{Q}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{P}_{X}^{\prime}
and that

\mathcal{P}_{X}
and
\mathcal{P}_{X}^{\prime}
agree on
\mathcal{Q}
. Thus,
\mathcal{Q}
is independent of
\mathcal{P}

and the composite
\mathcal{Q}\mathchoice{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\textstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}{\mathbin{\vbox{\hbox{\scalebox{.8}{$\scriptscriptstyle\bullet$}}}}}\mathcal{P}
witnesses
X\sim\mu~~*~~P
as desired.
∎

F.1.
Verification of one-time pad example

The one-time pad protocol is modelled by the following probabilistic program,
parameterized by a constant
m
representing the message being encrypted:

(
OneTimePad
)

\displaystyle K\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle C\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(K\oplus m)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~C

F.1.1.
Uniformity

Uniformity is specified by the triple

\{\top\}~\ref{prog:onetimepad}(m)~\{C.\,C\sim\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\}
.

This can be established by the following annotation:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\top\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptK\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptC\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(K\oplus m)\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~C\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}K\oplus m\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~C

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~C\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}K\oplus m\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~C\oplus m\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}K\}}

rearranging the equality

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~(C\oplus m)\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2\}}

\displaystyle\text{substituting away }K

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~C\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2\}}

Lemma
F.1

F.1.2.
Input independence

Input independence is specified by the triple

\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M\}~\ref{prog:onetimepad}(M)~\{C.\,\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~C\sim\operatorname{Ber}~1/2\}.

This can be established by the following annotation:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptK\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002ptC\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(K\oplus M)\hskip 1.00006pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~C\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}K\oplus M\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~C

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~C\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}K\oplus M\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~K\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~C\oplus M\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}K\}}

rearranging the equality

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{C.~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~(C\oplus M)\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2\}}

\displaystyle\text{substituting away }K

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{C.~\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{m\leftarrow M}((C\oplus M)\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)\right\}}

Lemma
F.4

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{C.~\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{m\leftarrow M}((C\oplus m)\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)\right\}}

\displaystyle\text{replacing }M\text{ with }m

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{C.~\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{m\leftarrow M}(C\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)\right\}}

F.1

F.2.
Verification of private information retrieval example

Let
i
be an
n
-tuple of boolean values with only a single component set to
T
.
The private information retrieval protocol is modelled by the following probabilistic program,
parameterized by
i
:

(
PrivateInformationRetrieval
)

\displaystyle Q_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle Q_{1}\leftarrow Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle A_{0}\leftarrow Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle A_{1}\leftarrow Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle R_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~\left(\bigoplus A_{0}\right)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle R_{1}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~\left(\bigoplus R_{1}\right)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle R\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0}\oplus R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R)

F.2.1.
Uniformity

Uniformity is specified by the triple

\{\top\}~\ref{prog:pir}(i)~\{(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R).\,~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~\land~~Q_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\}.

This can be established by the following annotation:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\top\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{1}\leftarrow Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptA_{0}\leftarrow Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptA_{1}\leftarrow Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~\left(\bigoplus A_{0}\right)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR_{1}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~\left(\bigoplus R_{1}\right)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0}\oplus R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~\\
&R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}~~*~~R\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\oplus R_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R).\hskip 4.62497pt\begin{aligned} &Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~\\
&R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}~~*~~R\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\oplus R_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 120.2492pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\vdots}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R).~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~\land~~Q_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\right\}}

The final entailment (hidden by the vertical ellipses) can be established as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\begin{aligned} &Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~\\
&R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}~~*~~R\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\oplus R_{1}\end{aligned}\right)

\displaystyle\vdash Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i

dropping some conjuncts

\displaystyle\vdash(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i)\land(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i)

\displaystyle\text{by }P\vdash P\land P

\displaystyle\vdash(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\land(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}i)

weakening first conjunct

\displaystyle\vdash(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)~~\land~~(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\oplus^{n}i\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0})

rearranging the equality

\displaystyle\vdash(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)~~\land~~((Q_{1}\oplus^{n}i)\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)

\displaystyle\text{substituting away }Q_{0}

\displaystyle\vdash(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)~~\land~~(Q_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)

Lemma
F.1

F.2.2.
Input independence

Let
I
be a random
n
-bitvector.
20
20

20

Following
Barthe et al. 2019
, we don’t even need require that
I

always have only a single component set to
T

to establish input independence.

Input independence is specified by the triple

\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I\}~\ref{prog:pir}(I)~\{C.\,(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I*\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}Q_{0})\land(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I*\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}Q_{1})\}.

This can be established by the following annotation:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptQ_{1}\leftarrow Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptA_{0}\leftarrow Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptA_{1}\leftarrow Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~\left(\bigoplus A_{0}\right)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR_{1}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~\left(\bigoplus R_{1}\right)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*\\
&R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}~~*~~R\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\oplus R_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0}\oplus R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*\\
&R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}~~*~~R\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\oplus R_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R)

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R).\hskip 4.62497pt\begin{aligned} &\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I~~*~~A_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*\\
&A_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{1}\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}\&\&}}^{n}d~~*~~R_{0}=\bigoplus A_{0}~~*~~R_{1}=\bigoplus A_{1}~~*~~R\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\oplus R_{1}\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R).~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 166.4989pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\vdots}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(Q_{0},Q_{1},A_{0},A_{1},R_{0},R_{1},R).~~(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I*\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}Q_{0})\land(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I*\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}Q_{1})\right\}}

The left conjunct of the postcondition follows from
Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}Q_{0}
and dropping the equality

Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I
.
The right conjunct is established by the following chain of entailments:

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}I~~*~~Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{i\leftarrow I}(Q_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~Q_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}Q_{0}\oplus^{n}I)

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{i\leftarrow I}(Q_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)

F.3.
Verification of oblivious transfer example

The oblivious transfer protocol is modelled by the following probabilistic program:

(
ObliviousTransfer
)

\displaystyle R_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle R_{1}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle D\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle(R_{D},R_{\neg D})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~D~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{1},R_{0})~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0},R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle E\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(c\oplus D)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle(F_{0},F_{1})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~E~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(m_{0}\oplus R_{1},m_{1}\oplus R_{0})~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(m_{0}\oplus R_{0},m_{1}\oplus R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle(m_{c},F_{\neg c})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~c~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(F_{1}\oplus R_{D},m_{0}\oplus R_{\neg D})~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(F_{0}\oplus R_{D},m_{1}\oplus R_{\neg D})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},m_{c},F_{\neg c})

This program is parameterized by the two messages
m_{0}
and
m_{1}
on offer and the bit
c
encoding
the receiver’s choice.

F.3.1.
Uniformity

Uniformity is specified by the triple

\left\{\top\right\}~\ref{prog:ot}(c,m_{0},m_{1})~\left\{\left(\begin{aligned} &R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},\\
&E,F_{0},F_{1},m_{c},F_{\neg c}\end{aligned}\right).\,~\begin{aligned} &((R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~E\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)~~\land\\
&(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~(R_{D},F_{\neg c})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2)\end{aligned}\right\}.

The next page gives an annotated program that establishes this specification.

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\top\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR_{0}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptR_{1}\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}^{n}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptD\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}flip}}~1/2\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt(R_{D},R_{\neg D})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~D~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{1},R_{0})~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0},R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994ptE\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(c\oplus D)\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})~~*~~E\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}c\oplus D\\
\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt(F_{0},F_{1})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~E~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(m_{0}\oplus R_{1},m_{1}\oplus R_{0})~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(m_{0}\oplus R_{0},m_{1}\oplus R_{1})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})~~*~~E\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}c\oplus D~~*~~\\
&F_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{0})~~*~~F_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{1})\\
\end{aligned}\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt(m_{c},F_{\neg c})\leftarrow\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}if}}~c~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}then}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(F_{1}\oplus R_{D},m_{0}\oplus R_{\neg D})~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}else}}~\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(F_{0}\oplus R_{D},m_{1}\oplus R_{\neg D})\hskip 0.92505pt;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\underbrace{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\begin{aligned} &R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})~~*~~E\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}c\oplus D~~*~~\\
&F_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{0})~~*~~F_{1}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{1})~~*~~\\
&m_{c}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~F_{1}\oplus R_{D}~\mathrm{else}~F_{0}\oplus R_{D})~~*~~F_{\neg c}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{\neg D}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{\neg D})\\
\end{aligned}\right\}}}_{P}}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}ret}}~(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},m_{c},F_{\neg c})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},m_{c},F_{\neg c}).~P\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 83.24945pt{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\vdots}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}{\bigg\{}(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},m_{c},F_{\neg c}).~\begin{aligned} &\overbrace{((R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~E\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)}^{Q}~~\land\\
&\underbrace{(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~(R_{D},F_{\neg c})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2)}_{R}\end{aligned}{\bigg\}}}

The final entailment (hidden by the vertical ellipses), abbreviated
P\vdash Q\land R
,
can be established as follows.
First, to show
P\vdash Q
,

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\vdash R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~E\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}c\oplus D

dropping conjuncts

\displaystyle\vdash R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~E\oplus c\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}D

rearranging the equality

\displaystyle\vdash R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~(E\oplus c)\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2

\displaystyle\text{substituting away }D

\displaystyle\vdash R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~E\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2

Lemma
F.1

\displaystyle\vdash(R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~E\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2

Lemma
F.2

\displaystyle=Q

Second,
P\vdash R
can be established by the following chain:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\vdash{\left({\begin{aligned} &R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})~~*~~\\
&F_{\neg c}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{\neg D}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{\neg D})\end{aligned}}\right)}

dropping some conjuncts

\displaystyle\vdash\dots

\displaystyle\vdash{\left({\begin{aligned} &R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&F_{\neg c}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}\oplus R_{\neg D}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1}\oplus R_{\neg D})\\
\end{aligned}}\right)}

\displaystyle\vdash{\left({\begin{aligned} &R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&F_{\neg c}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1})\oplus R_{\neg D}\\
\end{aligned}}\right)}

commuting conversion

\displaystyle\vdash{\left({\begin{aligned} &R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&(\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1})\oplus F_{\neg c}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{\neg D}\\
\end{aligned}}\right)}

\displaystyle\text{property of }\oplus

\displaystyle\vdash{\left({\begin{aligned} &R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&((\mathrm{if}~c~\mathrm{then}~m_{0}~\mathrm{else}~m_{1})\oplus F_{\neg c})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2\end{aligned}}\right)}

\displaystyle\text{substitute away }R_{\neg D}

\displaystyle\vdash{R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~F_{\neg c}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2}

Lemma
F.1

\displaystyle\vdash D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~(R_{D},F_{\neg c})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2

Lemma
F.2

\displaystyle=R

The entailment hidden by ellipses
establishes the mutual independence of
D
,
R_{D}
, and
R_{\neg D}
,
the key property that perfect secrecy hinges on. The proof goes by case analysis on
D
,
and is shown on the next page.

\displaystyle{\left({\begin{aligned} &R_{0}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{1}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})\end{aligned}}\right)}

\displaystyle\vdash\underbrace{\left({\begin{aligned} &(R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{0}~\mathrm{else}~R_{1})\end{aligned}}\right)}_{S[D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2]}

Lemma
F.2

\displaystyle\vdash S[D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2]\land S[D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2]

\displaystyle\text{property of }\land

\displaystyle\vdash S[D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2]\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

drop conjuncts

\displaystyle\vdash S[\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}D]\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

\displaystyle X\sim\mu\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X

\displaystyle\vdash{\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}\left((R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~\dots\right)}\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

Lemma
F.4

\displaystyle\vdash{\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}\underbrace{\left({\begin{aligned} &(R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~\\
&R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~d~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}\dots\end{aligned}}\right)}_{Q(d)}}\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

\displaystyle\text{replace }D\textrm{ with }d

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}\left(Q(\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}T}})\land Q(\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}F}})\right)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

\displaystyle\text{cases on }d

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}\bigg(\begin{aligned} &\overbrace{\left((R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}~~*~~R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{1}~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}\right)}^{Q(\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}T}})}\land\\
&\underbrace{\left((R_{0},R_{1})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2~~*~~R_{D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{0}~~*~~R_{\neg D}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}R_{1}\right)}_{Q(\texttt{{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,0.5}F}})}\end{aligned}\bigg)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

\displaystyle\text{unfold }Q

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}\bigg(((R_{\neg D},R_{D})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2)\land((R_{D},R_{\neg D})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2)\bigg)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

substitute

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}\bigg(\begin{aligned} &(R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\land\\
&(R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\end{aligned}\bigg)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

Lemma
F.2

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}(R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

\displaystyle\text{by }P\land P\vdash P

\displaystyle\vdash\D^{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}}_{d\leftarrow D}((R_{D},R_{\neg D})\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{2n}1/2)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

Lemma
F.2

\displaystyle\vdash(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}D~~*~~R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2)\land(D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2)

Lemma
F.3

\displaystyle\vdash D\sim\operatorname{Ber}1/2~~*~~R_{\neg D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2~~*~~R_{D}\sim\operatorname{Ber}^{n}1/2

Lemma
F.5

F.3.2.
Input independence

Following
Barthe et al. 2019
, the following Hoare triple specifies input
independence for
ObliviousTransfer
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(C,M_{0},M_{1})\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt{\ref{prog:ot}(M_{0},M_{1},C)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},M_{C},M_{1-C},F_{\neg C}).~\begin{aligned} &(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}C~~*~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(R_{0},R_{1},E))~~\land\\
&(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M_{1-C}~~*~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(D,R_{D},F_{0},F_{1}))\\
\end{aligned}\right\}}

In the postcondition,
M_{1-C}
denotes the random variable
(\mathrm{if}~C~\mathrm{then}~M_{0}~\mathrm{else}~M_{1})
.

Barthe et al. 2019
observe that the proof gets stuck, mentioning
that even an informal proof sketch does not seem easy.
We show that this triple is in fact
impossible to establish by giving an explicit counterexample. This takes the form of
three random variables
C
,
M_{0}
, and
M_{1}
such that
the postcondition fails.
In particular, we choose
C=0
and
M_{0}=M_{1}=M
for some uniformly-distributed
M
.
Now the triple reads

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(C,M,M)\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt{\ref{prog:ot}(M,M,C)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},M_{C},M_{1-C},F_{\neg C}).~\begin{aligned} &(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}C~~*~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(R_{0},R_{1},E))~~\land\\
&(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(D,R_{D},F_{0},F_{1}))\\
\end{aligned}\right\}}

Suppose this triple holds.
Then so does the following triple, where we have dropped the first conjunct of the postcondition:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(C,M,M)\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt{\ref{prog:ot}(M,M,C)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},M_{C},M_{1-C},F_{\neg C}).~(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(D,R_{D},F_{0},F_{1}))\right\}}

Lilac’s semantic model validates the following derived rule:

Lemma F.6.

Let
X
be a random variable and
f:\mathrm{cod}(X)\to B
measurable.
Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}X\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(f\circ X)
.

Proof.

The composition of measurable maps remains measurable.
∎

Thus we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(D,R_{D},F_{0},F_{1})\vdash\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(R_{D}\oplus F_{0})
,
so the following triple holds:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(C,M,M)\right\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 10.00002pt{\ref{prog:ot}(M,M,C)}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.5,0.1914,0.6563}\left\{(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},M_{C},M_{1-C},F_{\neg C}).~(\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}M~~*~~\operatorname{\mathsf{own}}(R_{D}\oplus F_{0}))\right\}}

At this point we transition from working in Lilac to reading off
the meanings of Lilac propositions in our semantic model.
We know that, by forward symbolic execution as in the previous section,
the OT protocol sets

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~M_{0}\oplus R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~M_{1}\oplus R_{0})

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}C\oplus D

\displaystyle R_{D}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})

We have set
M_{0}=M_{1}=M
and
C=0
for the sake of contradiction, so these equations become

\displaystyle F_{0}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}M\oplus(\mathrm{if}~E~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})

\displaystyle E

\displaystyle\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}D

\displaystyle R_{D}

\displaystyle\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})

Further substitution gives

F_{0}\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}M\oplus(\mathrm{if}~D~\mathrm{then}~R_{1}~\mathrm{else}~R_{0})\mathbin{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathsf{as}}}{{\mathop{=}}}}M\oplus R_{D}.

Reading the final Hoare triple obtained above in terms of the model, we have that

\llbracket\ref{prog:ot}\rrbracket
is a Markov kernel
whose pushforward along the distribution on
(C,M,M)

gives a distribution on

(R_{0},R_{1},D,R_{D},R_{\neg D},E,F_{0},F_{1},M_{C},M_{1-C},F_{\neg C})

in which
M
is independent of
(R_{D}\oplus F_{0})
.
But we also have

R_{D}\oplus F_{0}=R_{D}\oplus(M\oplus R_{D})=M

by the above deduction, so this Hoare triple asserts
the self-independence of
M
.
This is a contradiction: we have chosen
M
to be a uniformly-distributed
n
-tuple
of boolean values, which cannot be self-independent.

Intuitively, the special case
C=0
and
M_{0}=M_{1}=M
that we have chosen
is the situation where both messages offered by the sender are exactly the same
and the receiver always opts to receive message
0
. The failure of input
independence corresponds to the fact that the receiver manages to learn what
message
1
is. But it only learns message
1
because in this particular situation
the two messages happen to be the same! Thus this counterexample appears
to be more an issue with this particular specification of perfect secrecy via
input independence than a vulnerability in the OT protocol.
